Editor-Publisher of EDEN BULLETIN, has stood
virtually alone in his consistent public
advocacy of the vital UFO research of the
late Dr. Wilhelm Reich, who died in a federal
penitentiary in 1957 in defense of his work.
Eden was the first UFOlogist to offer docu­
mentary proof of the connection between
nation-\vide cattle mutilations and UFOs.
   No\v, in his latest EDEN BULLETIN, he offers
evidence linking Bigfoot and UFOs.

persP-ective. Your talents, experience, research into the Reich Cloud­
puster and overall persistence to attempt to warn the�blic of a futur�
global threat posed by UFOs have made you a legend in your own time.11

"Keep up your straightfor\vard work. "--Leonard Stringfield

"His work deserves serious consideration."--The Earl of Clancarty

author of Man in the Trap and President of The American College of
Orgonomy said of EDEN BULLETIN: "It is very important work, and one

                                HAZARDOUS MICROWAVE RADIATION
                                         DAVID E. WATERS (SBI· Field Investigator)


As is sometimes the case,        truth is stranger than fiction.                 Having been an investigator
of unexplained phenomena for the last fifteen years,                 I've never come across a more
striking example of this maxi!m. than the bizarre and,                as it turned out, deadly house
I investigated in 1968.


On the outskirts of Schenectady,        New York,         the city abruptly ceases and open,                    gentle
farm land surrounds a traveler quickly, as though going through a time warp.
Pri�cetown Road in Rotterdam is typical of this effect.                    At a relatively sparsely
populated intersection,      Mr. and Mrs. Eugene Binkowski and their family lived in a
                                                  I
house that could have been born in the mind of Edgar Allen Poe.                                Weird physical,
electrical and radio effects happening over a period of years to the Binkowski's
resulted in financial ruin,     illness,   and,       finally,   death.    The most amazing thing about
this case was an investigation and explanation by the United States Air Force of all
people.


When I first met the Binkowski's they had moved out of the afflicted house.                                Although
·much less affluent,     they were very friendly and helpful to me,                          a young and very green
investigator.      Unfortunat-ely ,   they were very ill.


In the beginning, they were very happy living in the house for some time without any
_trouble.    Mr.   Binkowski had a very good job at the General Electric plant in Sche­
nectady which allowed them to meet the mortgage payment every month.                                    His wife
stayed at home to care for the children who were in Schoof.                              There was never any
hint of a problem,     except for a little interference on their radio. and television from
a close WGY transmitter antenna.


The problem started in the late 50's,        shortly after a large metal commercial build­
ing was erected near this home.          The first indications they had of something amiss
were light bulbs occasionally exploded and an increasing number of electrj.cal mal­
functions,    e:specially motors would burn out much faster than· normal use would in­
dicate.     What fascinated visitors most was that almost anything electrical in nature
became a radio receiver picking up station WGY.


Even \veirder effects appeared that first winter.                At that time,               we had a typical
Northeastern winter,      cold with much snow.             However,       within six feet of the Binkow­
ski's house the snow never stayed,         and their heating bill dropped by 60o/o.                      At this
time,   they also noted very unusu2.l but be. .BUtiful crystal growths in their cellar.
Visitors,    as well as the family pets, who· stayed inside normally,                           didn't like to re­
main in the house for very long ev.en in the worst weather.


The visitors said that "they didn't like the feel of the place               .   .   •   .   it made them nervous. 11
At t�is point the Binkowski's started to notice the biological changes in themselves.
They became nervous and irritable almost all the time including the children.                                   But

started to become very sickly.         The increasing bouts of various ills started to affect
Mr.   Binkowski so much it threatened his job.
